53.96643pt\raise-18.39351pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.2646pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{ad_{j^{*},j_{*}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 87.3361pt\raise-24.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 87.3361pt\raise-24.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{j_{*}j^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 99.62383pt\raise-42.59418pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-9.20903pt\raise-49.98004pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{i_{!}i^{!}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 15.27457pt\raise-43.86894pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\beta_{i}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 33.20903pt\raise-49.98004pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 33.20903pt\raise-49.98004pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{i_{*}i^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 79.29375pt\raise-49.98004pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 79.29375pt\raise-49.98004pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{i_{*}i^{*}j_{*}j^{*}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{split}

Each arrow in (
4.1.12.a
) is a unit or counit for one of the adjunctions

(k^{*},k_{*})
or
(k_{!},k^{!})
,
k=i,j
.
The second and third rows (resp. columns) are the classical localization triangle,
expressed in terms of natural transformation.
In particular,
each row and column of (
4.1.12.a
) is exact homotopy
(concretely: gives a homotopy exact sequence in
\T(X)
when evaluated at any object).

Note, moreover, that
\alpha_{j}
is given by the map
j_{!}\rightarrow j_{*}
”forgetting the support” where the map
\beta_{i}
corresponds to a natural transformation
\beta_{i}:i^{!}\rightarrow i^{*}
which is specific to
the case of (closed)
immersions.
8
8

8

It can also be derived from the exchange transformation
i^{!}Id^{*}\rightarrow i^{*}Id^{!}
.

Note, finally, the classical identification of
functors
i^{!}j_{!}=i^{*}j_{*}[-1]

obtained by applying the localization triangles (middle row of the previous diagram)
post-composed with
j_{!}j^{!}
to get the homotopy exact sequence

i_{!}i^{!}j_{!}j^{!}\rightarrow j_{!}j^{!}\rightarrow j_{*}j^{*}j_{!}j^{!}=j_{*}j^{*}

Since the last arrow identifies with
\alpha_{j}
, one gets
i_{!}i^{!}j_{!}j^{!}=i_{*}i^{*}j_{*}j^{*}
,
which gives the result since
i_{*}=i_{!}
(resp.
j^{*}=j^{!}
) is right invertible.

We thus obtain the following expression of the punctured tubular neighborhood.

Proposition 4.1.13
.

There is a canonical isomorphism

\operatorname{TN}^{\times}_{S}(X,Z)\simeq p_{!}i^{!}j_{!}q^{!}(\mathbf{1}_{S})=p_{!}i^{*}j_{*}q^{!}(\mathbf{1}_{S})[-1]

This relation explains the close connection between punctured tubular neighborhoods and nearby cycles.
In this line of thought, we extend
[
87
, Theorem 5.1]
and
[
32
, 1.4.6]
to our context.

Theorem 4.1.14
.

Let
S
be an excellent scheme, and let
(X,Z)
,
(Y,T)
be closed
S
-pairs.
Assume that there exists an isomorphism
f:T\rightarrow Z
,
which extends to an isomorphism of the respective formal completions

\mathfrak{f}:\hat{Y}_{T}\rightarrow\hat{X}_{Z}
. Then there exists a canonical isomorphism

\mathfrak{f}^{*}:\operatorname{TN}^{\times}_{S}(Y,T)\xrightarrow{\simeq}\operatorname{TN}^{\times}_{S}(X,Z)

which is compatible with composition in
\mathfrak{f}
.

Proof.

We can assume that
Z=T
and that
Z
is reduced.
It suffices to build an isomorphism

\tilde{\mathfrak{f}}^{*}:\operatorname{TN}^{\times}_{S}(Y,T)\rightarrow\operatorname{TN}^{\times}_{S}(X,Z)

and a commutative diagram

\textstyle{\Pi_{S}(Y/Y-Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\mathfrak{f}^{*}}}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Pi_{S}(X/X-Z)}

We can apply the strategy of the proof of
[
32
, Theorem 1.4.6]
using Artin’s approximation theorem at points of
Z

(here, we use the assumption that
S
is excellent)
and Zariski hypercovers to globalize the situation.
Here we do not need to extend our motivic category to diagrams of base schemes.
The proof proceeds with the simplicial schemes corresponding to Zariski hypercoverings directly within the

\infty
-category
\T(S)
.
∎

4.2.
Punctured tubular neighborhood of subschemes with crossing singularities

Based on
3.2.3
, we now state our main tool for computing punctured tubular neighborhoods of h-smooth crossing subschemes (
3.3.2
). We adopt the notation of
3.3.1

and
3.3.9
.

Theorem 4.2.1
.

Let
(X,Z)
be a closed
S
-pair such that
Z/S
has h-smooth crossings over
S

and
X/S
is h-smooth in a Nisnevich neighborhood of
Z
and let
v
be a virtual vector bundle on
X
.

Then
\operatorname{TN}^{\times}_{S}(X,Z,v)
is canonically isomorphic to the
homotopy fiber of the map

\colim_{n\in(\Delta^{\mathrm{inj}})^{op}}\left(\bigoplus_{J\subset I,\sharp J=n+1}\Pi_{S}(Z_{J},v_{J})\right)\xrightarrow{\partial}\underset{n\in\Delta^{\mathrm{inj}}}{\lim}\left(\bigoplus_{J\subset I,\sharp J=m+1}\Pi_{S}(Z_{J},v_{J}+\langle N_{J}\rangle)\right)

Here the direct images define the face maps

(\delta_{n}^{k})_{*}=\sum_{K=\{i_{0}<\ldots<i_{n}\},J=\{i_{0}<\ldots<\not{i_{k}}<\ldots<i_{n}\}}(\nu_{K}^{J})_{*}

in the source, and the Gysin maps define the coface maps

(\tilde{\delta}^{m}_{l})^{!}=\sum_{K=\{i_{0}<\ldots<i_{m}\},J=\{i_{0}<\ldots<\not{i_{l}}<\ldots<i_{m}\}}(\nu_{K}^{J})^{!}

in the target.
Moreover,

\partial
is induced by the canonical map in degree zero

(4.2.1.a)

(\delta_{ij}=\bar{\nu}_{j}^{!}\bar{\nu}_{i*})_{i,j\in I}\colon\bigoplus_{i\in I}\Pi_{S}(Z_{i},v_{i})\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{j\in I}\Pi_{S}(Z_{j},v_{j}+\langle N_{j}\rangle)

Finally, using the Euler class
e(N_{i}):\mathbf{1}_{Z_{i}}\rightarrow\Th(N_{i})
(see paragraph
2.3.3
)
of the normal bundle
N_{i}
,
one can compute the diagonal coefficients of this matrix as

\delta_{ii}=p_{i!}\big(e(N_{i})\otimes\Th(\tau_{i}+v_{i})\big)

where
p_{i}:Z_{i}\rightarrow S
is the (h-smooth) projection,
with virtual tangent bundle
\tau_{i}
.

Proof.

According to
4.1.1
,
we have to compute the homotopy fiber of the map

\beta_{X,Z}:\Pi_{S}(Z,v)\rightarrow\Pi_{S}(X/X-Z,v)

3.3.3
identifies
\beta_{X,Z}
’s source with the desired colimit whereas
3.3.10
identifies its target with the desired limit. The computation of the (co)face maps and of
\partial
follows from these two propositions.
The final remark follows from the definition of
\tilde{\mu}_{ii}
,
the excess intersection formula
[
35
, Proposition 3.2.8]
,
and
p_{i}^{!}(\mathbf{1}_{S})\simeq\Th(\tau_{i})

since
p_{i}
is h-smooth by assumption.
∎

One can suggestively summarize the computation in
4.2.1
with the diagram

\displaystyle\left(\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 39.9511pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\cr&\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 120.37741pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-39.9511pt\raise-14.875pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\bigoplus_{i_{1}<i_{2}}\Pi_{S}(Z_{i_{1}i_{2}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.0pt\raise-24.875pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-4.0pt\raise-24.875pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 120.37741pt\raise-14.875pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-30.92969pt\raise-32.875pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\bigoplus_{i\in I}\Pi_{S}(Z_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 50.1378pt\raise-27.44444pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\partial}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 79.61638pt\raise-32.875pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 79.61638pt\raise-32.875pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\bigoplus_{j\in I}\Pi_{S}(Z_{j},\langle N_{j}\rangle)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 127.37741pt\raise-42.875pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 119.37741pt\raise-42.875pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-50.875pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 63.9511pt\raise-50.875pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\bigoplus_{j_{1}<j_{2}}\Pi_{S}(Z_{j_{1}j_{2}},\langle N_{j_{1}j_{2}}\rangle)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-65.75pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 120.37741pt\raise-65.75pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}}\right)

Typically,
a punctured tubular neighborhood computation will consist of determining the homotopy colimit
(resp. limit) of the left (resp. right) column and then determining the map
\partial
.
For a closed
S
-pair
(X,Z)
such that
X
is smooth over
S
in a Nisnevich neighborhood of
Z
,

\tau_{X/S}
is a well-defined virtual vector bundle on a suitable Nisnevich neighborhood of
Z
,
and its restriction
i^{-1}\tau_{X/S}
to
Z
is a well-defined virtual vector bundle on
Z
,
see
2.4.2
.
Since the twisted punctured tubular neighborhood of
Z
in
X
depends only on a Nisnevich neighborhood of
Z
in
X
,
the object
\operatorname{TN}^{\times}_{S}(X,Z,-v-\tau_{X/S})
is well-defined for every virtual vector bundle
v
on
(a Nisnevich neighborhood of
Z
in)
X
.
One derives from
3.4.3
the following strong duality result.

Theorem 4.2.2
.

Let
(X,Z)
be a closed
S
-pair such that
X
is smooth in a Nisnevich neighbordhood of
Z
and such
Z/S
is proper with smooth crossings over
S
. Then for every virtual vector bundle
v
on
X
,
\operatorname{TN}^{\times}_{S}(X,Z,v)
is rigid with dual
\operatorname{TN}^{\times}_{S}(X,Z,-v-\tau_{X/S})[-1]
.

In particular, under the stated hypothesis, the punctured tubular neighborhood
\operatorname{TN}^{\times}(X,Z)
is auto-dual, up to twist and shift.

4.3.
Stable homotopy at infinity and boundary motives

As explained in the next examples,
the following definition is rooted in both classical topology, see
[
56
]
,
and in Wildeshaus’ theory of boundary motives
[
88
]
.

Definition 4.3.1
.

The
homotopy at infinity
of a separated
S
-scheme
X/S
is the homotopy fiber computed in
\T(S)
of the map
\alpha_{X/S}:\Pi_{S}(X)\to\Pi_{S}^{c}(X)
in
(
2.2.1.a
) so that there is a homotopy exact sequence

\Pi_{S}^{\infty}(X)\longrightarrow\Pi_{S}(X)\xrightarrow{\alpha_{X/S}}\Pi_{S}^{c}(X)

Owing to (
1.2.0.a
),
the main case is
\T=\SH
.
We refer to the spectrum
\Pi^{\infty}_{S}(X)
in
\SH(S)
as the
stable homotopy at infinity
of
X
relative to
S
.

Example 4.3.2
.

Let
p:V\rightarrow S
be a vector bundle and
consider the closed pair
(V,S)
given by the zero section
s:S\to V
. Then, using purity isomorphisms, one gets the commutative diagram

\textstyle{\Pi_{S}(V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{V/S}}
\scriptstyle{p_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{can}
\textstyle{\Pi_{S}^{c}(V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{p_{*}p^{!}(\mathbf{1}_{S})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{p}_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\mathbf{1}_{S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e(V)}
\textstyle{p_{*}(\Th_{V}(p^{-1}V))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{p_{*}p^{*}(\Th_{S}(V))}
\textstyle{\Pi_{S}(V/V-Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{p}_{V,S}}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\Th_{S}(V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{ad_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\sim}

The fact that
p_{*}
and the unit
ad_{p}
are isomorphisms follows from
\mathbf{A}^{1}
-homotopy invariance.
The purity isomorphism
\mathfrak{p}_{p}
exists since
p
is smooth,
and
\mathfrak{p}_{V,S}
is the (tautological) purity isomorphism.
The right-hand side commutes by applying
[
35
, Lemma 3.3.1]
to
f=p
,
i=s
,
i^{\prime}=Id_{V}
,
while commutativity of the left-hand side follows by definition of the Euler class
e(V)
(
2.3.3
).
We deduce the homotopy exact sequence

\Pi_{S}^{\infty}(V)\rightarrow\mathbf{1}_{S}\xrightarrow{e(V)}\Th(V)

In other words,

\Pi_{S}^{\infty}(V)=\Pi_{S}(V^{\times})
and, if
e(V)=0
then
\Pi_{S}^{\infty}(V)=\mathbf{1}_{S}\oplus\Th(V)[-1]

It follows from the discussion in Section
1.2

that
\Pi^{\infty}_{S}(X)
realizes to the analogous definition for the other motivic
\infty
-categories of
(
1.2.0.a
).

Example 4.3.3
.

Motivic realization
.

Let
S
be the spectrum of a perfect field
k
of characteristic exponent
p
and let
X
be a separated
k
-scheme. Then the motivic realization functor (see also
[
53
]
,
[
79
]
in this case)

(4.3.3.a)

\SH(k)\rightarrow\DM(k)[1/p]

sends
\Pi_{k}(X)
to Voevodsky’s homological motive
M(X)
of
X
(
[
25
, §8.7]
),
and it sends
\Pi_{k}^{c}(X)
to
M^{c}(X)
,
Voevodsky’s homological motive of
X
with compact support (
[
25
, Proposition 8.10]
).
It follows that the motivic realization functor sends
\Pi_{k}^{\infty}(X)
to
the boundary motive

\partial M(X)
of
X
(see Wildeshaus
[
87
]
).
In particular,
the Betti or
\ell
-adic cohomology of
\Pi_{k}^{\infty}(X)
coincides with the so-called

interior cohomology
of
X
.
We generalize the above discussion to arbitrary base schemes in
Section

5
.

Example 4.3.4
.

Betti realization
.

Let
S
be the spectrum of a field
k
which admits a complex embedding
\sigma
, and consider the Betti realization functor (see Section
1.2
)

(4.3.4.a)

\SH(k)\rightarrow\DB(k)=\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z})

Owing to Ayoub’s enhancement of (
4.3.4.a
) to an arbitrary base scheme using the technique
of analytical sheaves
[
12
]
,
one derives that for any separated
k
-scheme
X
,
the spectrum
\Pi_{k}(X)
realizes to the singular chain complex
S_{*}(X^{\sigma})
of the analytification
X^{\sigma}
of
X
,
and the spectrum
\Pi_{k}^{c}(X)
realizes to the Borel-Moore singular chain complex
S_{*}^{BM}(X^{\sigma})
.
As
X^{\sigma}
is locally contractible and
\sigma
-compact,
the latter is quasi-isomorphic to the complex
S_{*}^{lf}(W)
of locally finite singular chains
(
[
56
, Chapter 3]
).
Thus the stable homotopy type at infinity
\Pi_{S}^{\infty}(X)
realizes to the singular complex at infinity

S_{*}^{\infty}(X^{\sigma})
(see Definition
[
56
]
),
defined by the distinguished triangle of chain complexes of abelian groups

(4.3.4.b)

S^{\infty}_{*}(X^{\sigma})\rightarrow S_{*}(X^{\sigma})\xrightarrow{\alpha_{X^{\sigma}}}S_{*}^{lf}(X^{\sigma})\rightarrow S^{\infty}_{*}(X^{\sigma})[1]

As a corollary of
2.6.4
, we get the following computations:

Proposition 4.3.5
.

In the setting of
2.6.4
assume that either i) or ii) holds and that
Y/S
is proper. Then there is a canonical isomorphism

\Pi_{S}^{\infty}(X\times_{S}Y)\simeq\Pi_{S}^{\infty}(X)\otimes\Pi_{S}(Y)

Proposition 4.3.6
.

In the setting of
2.6.4
assume that
g:Y\to S
is smooth and stably
\mathbf{A}^{1}
-contractible over
S
with relative tangent bundle
T_{g}
stably constant over
S
and let
v_{0}
be a virtual vector bundle over
S
such that

\langle T_{g}\rangle=g^{*}v_{0}
in
K_{0}(Y)
.
Then there exists a homotopy exact sequence

\Pi^{\infty}_{S}(X\times_{S}Y)\longrightarrow\Pi_{S}(X)\xrightarrow{\alpha_{X}\otimes\alpha_{Y}}\Pi_{S}^{c}(X)\otimes\Th(v_{0})

In particular,
if
T_{g}
is the pullback of a vector bundle
V
over
S
with a trivial Euler class,
then

\Pi^{\infty}_{S}(X\times_{S}Y)=\Pi_{S}(X)\oplus\Pi_{S}^{c}(X)\otimes\Th_{S}(V)[-1]

Note that the splitting uses
4.3.2
.

Example 4.3.7
.

Let
X
be a smooth stably
\mathbf{A}^{1}
-contractible variety of dimension
d
over a field
k
. Then,
4.3.6
implies that

\Pi^{\infty}_{k}(X)=\mathbf{1}_{k}\oplus\mathbf{1}_{k}(d)[2d-1]=\Pi^{\infty}_{k}(\mathbf{A}^{d}_{k})

In other words,
stable homotopy at infinity cannot distinguish between
X
and affine space
\mathbf{A}^{d}_{k}
,
as one would expect from topology (see
[
7
]
).
A theory of unstable motivic homotopy at infinity, however, is expected to provide a finer invariant.

Similarly, the situation for smooth morphisms
f:X\rightarrow S
with stably
\mathbf{A}^{1}
-contractible fibers over a general base
S
is entirely described by their stable tangent bundles. In particular,
if
T_{f}
is constant over
S
, equal to
f^{*}V
for some vector bundle
V
on
S
, then the stable homotopy type at infinity of
X
is the same as that of the vector bundle
V
. It is thus essentially described by the Euler class of
V
as explained in
4.3.2
.

Remark 4.3.8
.

In general,
one can interpret
\Pi^{\infty}_{S}(X)
as an extension of
\Pi_{S}(X)
by
\Pi_{S}^{c}(X)
.
This viewpoint is prominent in Wildeshaus’ work on boundary motives;
a motivic realization,
where weight considerations are at stake.
In topology,
it is well-known that forming a product with Euclidean space
\mathbf{R}^{n}
kills the fundamental group at infinity.
In our stable context,
taking a product with affine space
\mathbf{A}^{n}
,
or more generally, any smooth stably
\mathbf{A}^{1}
-contractible
S
-scheme
f:Y\to S
of relative dimension
n
with a trivial relative tangent bundle splits the extension in the sense that

\Pi^{\infty}_{S}(X\times Y)\simeq\Pi_{S}(X)\oplus\Pi_{S}^{c}(X)(n)[2n-1]

As an application of the results and techniques above, we can now wholly determine the homotopy at infinity of complements of stably
\mathbf{A}^{1}
-contractible arrangements in smooth stably
\mathbf{A}^{1}
-contractible schemes over a field (see
3.5.1
).

Proposition 4.3.9
.

Let
S
be a smooth stably
\mathbf{A}^{1}
-contractible scheme over a field
k
and let
(X,Z)
be a stably
\mathbf{A}^{1}
-contractible arrangement over
S
such that
Z
is a normal crossing closed subscheme of
X
. Then there exists a canonical isomorphism

\Pi^{\infty}_{S}(X-Z)\simeq\bigoplus_{i=0}^{d}m(i)\mathbf{1}_{S}(i)[i]\oplus\bigoplus_{j=0}^{d}m(j)\mathbf{1}_{S}(d-j)[2d-j-1]

where
d
is the dimension of
X
over
S
and where
m(n)
denotes the sum of the number of connected components of all codimension
n
subschemes
Z_{J}
of
X
.

Proof.

Indeed, applying
3.5.3
and
3.5.4
,
we deduce the homotopy exact sequence

\Pi_{S}^{\infty}(X)\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{i=0}^{d}m(i)\mathbf{1}_{S}(i)[i]\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{j=0}^{d}m(j)\mathbf{1}_{S}(d-j)[2d-j]

To conclude,
it suffices to prove that the second map is zero.
Since
S
is stably
\mathbf{A}^{1}
-contractible over the field
k
, it is given by a sum of elements of the groups

\pi^{2d-i-j,d-i-j}(k)
. Since
d>0
, these groups are all trivial by Morel’s stable
\mathbf{A}^{1}
-connectivity theorem.
∎

4.4.
Stable homotopy type at infinity via punctured tubular neighborhoods

4.4.1
.

Recall that a
compactification
of a separated morphism of finite type
f\colon X\to S
consists of
an open immersion
j:X\hookrightarrow\bar{X}
into a proper
S
-scheme
\bar{f}\colon\bar{X}\to S
.
The closed subscheme
\partial X=(\bar{X}-X)_{red}
of
\bar{X}
is called the
boundary
of the compactification
j
.
We denote by
i:\partial X\hookrightarrow\bar{X}
the corresponding closed immersion and set

\partial\bar{f}=\bar{f}\circ i:\partial X\to S
in the commutative diagram

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\bar{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{f}}
\textstyle{\partial X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\scriptstyle{\partial\bar{f}}
\textstyle{S}

The following result gives our main tool for computing stable homotopy types at infinity.
For specializations to topology and motives,
see
[
56
]
and
[
88
, Theorem 1.6]
,
respectively.

Proposition 4.4.2
.

Let
(\bar{X},\partial X)
be the closed
S
-pair associated with a compactification of a separated
S
-scheme of
finite type. Then there exists a canonical isomorphism

\Pi^{\infty}_{S}(X)\simeq\operatorname{TN}^{\times}_{S}(\bar{X},\partial X)

which is natural in
(\bar{X},X,\partial X)
,
covariantly functorial with respect to proper maps,
and contravariantly functorial with respect to étale maps.

Proof.

Given the six functors formalism, this is a direct application of
4.1.6
. More precisely, with the notation of
4.4.1
,
one reduces to the commutative diagram

\textstyle{f_{!}f^{!}(\mathbf{1}_{S})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{f_{*}f^{!}(\mathbf{1}_{S})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\bar{f}_{*}j_{!}j^{!}\bar{f}^{!}(\mathbf{1}_{S})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{ad^{\prime}_{j_{!},j^{!}}}
\textstyle{\bar{f}_{*}\bar{f}^{!}(\mathbf{1}_{S})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{ad_{j_{*},j^{*}}}
\textstyle{\bar{f}_{*}j_{*}j^{*}\bar{f}^{!}(\mathbf{1}_{S})}

and exactness of the rows and columns of (
4.1.12.a
).
∎

Remark 4.4.3
.

The above result has the following geometric interpretations. First, using the notations of
4.1.6
for the closed
S
-pair
(\bar{X},\partial X)
and that of

4.3.1
,
the commutative diagram in the proof of
4.4.2
can be recast as

\textstyle{\Pi_{S}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{X}}
\textstyle{\Pi^{c}_{S}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\Pi_{S}(\bar{X}-\partial X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{\bar{X},\partial X}}
\textstyle{\Pi_{S}(\bar{X}/\partial X)}

In particular,
considering the Borel-Moore homotopy
\Pi_{S}^{c}(X)
of
X
naturally leads to considering the object
\bar{X}/\partial X

obtained by identifying the boundary
\partial X
of any compactification
\bar{X}
with a point.
The latter can be viewed as a motivic model for the one-point compactification in topology.

Second,
\Pi_{S}^{\infty}(X)
can be canonically identified with the homotopy fiber of the canonical map

(4.4.3.a)

\Pi_{S}(\partial X)\oplus\Pi_{S}(X)\xrightarrow{i_{*}+j_{*}}\Pi_{S}(\bar{X})

Under motivic realization,
(
4.4.3.a
) becomes the formula for the boundary motive given in
[
87
, Proposition 2.4]
.

A reformulation of
4.4.2
yields the following invariance result for the punctured tubular neighborhood of a closed subscheme
Z
of a proper
S
-scheme
X
:

Corollary 4.4.4
.

Let
(X,Z)
be a closed
S
-pair such that
X/S
is proper.
Then the punctured tubular neighborhood
\operatorname{TN}^{\times}_{S}(X,Z)
is isomorphic to
\Pi^{\infty}_{S}(X-Z)
,
and therefore it depends only on the open subscheme
X-Z
.

By combining

4.1.5
and
4.4.2
, we obtain the following result.

Corollary 4.4.5
.

Let
(\bar{X},\partial X)
be the closed
S
-pair associated to a compactification of a separated
S
-scheme
X
.
Assume that
(\bar{X},\partial X)
is weakly h-smooth with normal bundle
N=N_{\partial X/\bar{X}}
.
Then there is a homotopy exact sequence

(4.4.5.a)

\Pi_{S}^{\infty}(X)\longrightarrow\Pi_{S}(\partial X)\xrightarrow{e(N)}\Th_{S}(N)

where
e(N)
is induced by the Euler class of
N
(see
2.3.3
).
In particular,

\Pi_{S}^{\infty}(X)=\Pi_{S}(N^{\times})
,
and when
e(N)
vanishes,
there is a splitting
\Pi_{S}^{\infty}(X)=\Pi_{S}(\partial X)\oplus\Th_{S}(N)[-1]
.

Remark 4.4.6
.

Assume that
S
is the spectrum of a perfect field
k
of characteristic exponent
p
. Then the realization in
\DM(k)[1/p]

of the homotopy exact sequence (
4.4.5.a
)
is the homotopy exact sequence

\partial M(X)\longrightarrow M(\partial X)\xrightarrow{\tilde{c}_{r}(N)}M(\partial X)(r)[2r]

where
\partial M(X)
is the boundary motive of
X
in Example
4.3.3
,
r
is the rank of the normal bundle
N
of
\partial_{X}
in
\bar{X}
and the map
\tilde{c}_{r}(N)

is induced by multiplication with the top Chern class

c_{r}(N)\in\mathrm{CH}^{r}(\partial X)\simeq\Hom(M(\partial X),\mathbf{1}(r)[2r])
.
4.4.5
implies
that
\Pi_{k}^{\infty}(X)
is a strictly finer invariant than
\partial M(X)
, see
4.1.4
.

4.5.
Interpretation in terms of fundamental classes

In what follows,
we observe connections between stable homotopy at infinity
and more generally punctured tubular neighborhoods and certain fundamental classes.

Proposition 4.5.1
.

Let
f:X\rightarrow S
be a smooth morphism with relative tangent bundle
T_{f}
.
Then the map
\alpha^{\prime}_{X/S}
obtained by adjunction from the composite

\Pi_{S}(X)\xrightarrow{\alpha_{X/S}}\Pi^{c}_{S}(X)\simeq\uHom\big(\Pi_{S}(X,-T_{f}),\mathbf{1}_{S}\big),

where the isomorphism uses
2.5.5
(4),
fits into the commutative diagram

\textstyle{\Pi_{S}(X)\otimes\Pi_{S}(X,-T_{f})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha^{\prime}_{X/S}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\mathbf{1}_{S}}
\textstyle{\Pi_{S}(X\times_{S}X,-p_{j}^{-1}T_{f})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta^{!}}
\textstyle{\Pi_{S}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}

The left vertical map is the Künneth isomorphism (
2.6.1.b
) and
\delta^{!}
is the Gysin map
(
2.3.1
) associated with the diagonal immersion
\delta:X\rightarrow X\times_{S}X
.

In other words, the map
\alpha_{X/S}
, whose homotopy cofiber is the stable homotopy at infinity of
X/S
,
can be computed under the canonical isomorphisms

\displaystyle[\Pi_{S}(X),\Pi^{c}_{S}(X)]

\displaystyle\simeq[\Pi_{S}(X)\otimes\Pi_{S}(X,-T_{f}),\mathbf{1}_{S}]

\displaystyle\simeq[\Pi_{S}(X\times_{S}X),\Th(p_{j}^{-1}T_{f})]=H_{\T}^{0}(X\times_{S}X,p_{j}^{-1}(T_{f}))

as the twisted fundamental class
[\Delta_{X/S}]_{X\times X}^{j}
of the diagonal, with respect to
the
\delta
-parallelization corresponding to the smooth retraction
p_{j}
of
\delta
, see
2.3.6
.

Proof.

For notational convenience,
let
p_{1}:X\times_{S}X\rightarrow X
be the projection on the first factor. The associativity formula in

[
35
, Theorem 3.3.2]
shows the equality of fundamental classes
\eta_{\delta}.\eta_{p_{1}}=1
.
The assumption that
f
is smooth implies the cartesian square

\textstyle{X\times_{S}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\Delta}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{S}

is Tor-independent. Thus the transversal base change formula in
[
35
, Theorem 3.3.2]
implies the equality
\Delta^{*}(\eta_{f})=\eta_{p_{1}}

from which the commutativity of the square follows.
∎

Remark 4.5.2
.

Computing fundamental classes of the diagonal is a famous problem, at the center of the Chow-Künneth conjecture, for example.
The previous proposition shows the link between determining the stable homotopy type at infinity,
or the boundary motive, of
X/S
and computing the (twisted) fundamental class of its diagonal. The main difference with the Chow-Künneth conjecture
is that we are interested mainly in the non-proper case.

Similarly, one gets the following link between punctured tubular neighborhoods and another fundamental class.

Proposition 4.5.3
.

Let
(X,Z)
be a closed
S
-pair such that
X/S
is smooth with relative tangent bundle
T_{X/s}
and such that
Z/S
is proper and has smooth crossings (see
3.3.2
).
Then the map
\beta^{\prime}_{X,Z}
obtained by adjunction from

\Pi_{S}(Z)\xrightarrow{\beta_{X,Z}}\Pi_{S}(X/X-Z)\simeq\Pi_{S}(Z,-\langle i^{-1}T_{X/S}\rangle)^{\vee},

where the isomorphism follows from
4.2.2
, fits into the commutative diagram

\textstyle{\Pi_{S}(Z)\otimes\Pi_{S}(Z,-\langle i^{-1}T_{X/S}\rangle)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta^{\prime}_{X,Z}}
\scriptstyle{Id\otimes i_{*}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{1}_{S}}
\textstyle{\Pi_{S}(Z)\otimes\Pi_{S}(X,-\langle T_{X/S}\rangle)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{(*)}
\textstyle{\Pi_{S}(Z\times_{S}X,-\langle p_{j}^{-1}T_{X/S}\rangle)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{i}^{!}}
\textstyle{\Pi_{S}(Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q_{*}}

where
\gamma_{i}^{!}
is the Gysin morphism associated to the graph immersion
\gamma_{i}=Id\times i:Z\rightarrow Z\times_{S}X
.

In other words, the map
\beta_{X,Z}
, whose cone is the punctured tubular neighborhood
\operatorname{TN}^{\times}_{S}(X,Z)
of the pair
(X,Z)
,
can be computed under the canonical isomorphisms

\displaystyle[\Pi_{S}(Z),\Pi_{S}(X/X-Z)]

\displaystyle\simeq[\Pi_{S}(Z),\Pi_{S}(Z,-\langle i^{-1}T_{X/S}\rangle)^{\vee}]\simeq[\Pi_{S}(Z)\otimes\Pi_{S}(Z,-\langle i^{-1}T_{X/S}\rangle),\mathbf{1}_{S}]

\displaystyle\simeq[\Pi_{S}(Z\times_{S}X,-\langle p_{j}^{-1}T_{X/S}\rangle,\mathbf{1}_{S}]\simeq H_{\T}^{0}(Z\times_{S}X,p_{j}^{-1}T_{X/S})

as the twisted fundamental class
[\Gamma_{i}]_{Z\times X}^{can}
of the graph
\gamma_{i}

of the closed immersion
i:Z\to X
, with obvious
\gamma_{i}
-parallelization

N_{\gamma_{i}}\simeq\gamma_{i}^{-1}(p_{j}^{-1}T_{X/S})
.

Proof.

First, let us note that
\gamma_{i}
is a section of the smooth separated morphism

Z\times_{S}X\rightarrow Z
.
So it is a regular closed immersion whose normal bundle is isomorphic to the relative tangent bundle
p_{j}^{*}T_{X/S}
of
Z\times_{S}X
over
Z
. This justifies the existence of the Gysin map
\gamma_{i}^{!}
using
2.3.1
.
Secondly, the isomorphism (
*
) follows from the Künneth isomorphism of
3.3.6
.
A routine check using the definitions of the maps shows that the diagram commutes.
∎

4.5.4
.

Pushing the idea from the preceding result, one obtains a method of computation
for the decomposition of punctured tubular neighborhoods obtained in
4.2.1
.
We use the notations of
op. cit.
:
(X,Z)
is a closed
S
-pair,
Z=\cup_{i\in I}Z_{i}
.
Furthermore, we make the following assumptions.

(1)

X/S
is smooth with relative tangent bundle
T_{X/S}
.

(2)

Z/S
is proper and has smooth crossings.

In fact, as
Z_{i}/S
is smooth and proper, one deduces from
2.5.7
that

\Pi_{S}(Z_{i},N_{i})
is rigid with dual
\Pi_{S}(Z_{i},-\langle T_{X/S}^{i}\rangle)
, where we denote by
T_{X/S}^{i}

the restriction of
T_{X/S}
to
Z_{i}
and use the isomorphism of virtual vector bundles

\langle T_{X/S}^{i}\rangle=\langle N_{i}\rangle+\langle T_{Z_{i}/S}\rangle
.
Combined with the Künneth formula (
2.6.1.b
),
one gets a canonical isomorphism

(4.5.4.a)

\varphi:[\Pi_{S}(Z_{i}),\Pi_{S}(Z_{j},N_{j})]\xrightarrow{\ \cong\ }H_{\T}^{0}(Z_{i}\times_{S}Z_{j},p_{2}^{-1}T_{X/S}^{j})

Proposition 4.5.5
.

Consider the above assumptions and the cartesian square of closed immersions

(4.5.5.a)

\textstyle{Z^{\prime}_{ij}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu^{\prime}_{ij}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\textstyle{Z_{i}\times_{S}Z_{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{\nu}_{i}\times_{S}\bar{\nu}_{j}}
\textstyle{X\times_{S}X}

Let
\delta_{ij}:\Pi_{S}(Z_{i})\rightarrow\Pi_{S}(Z_{j},N_{j})
be the map appearing in
4.2.1
.

(1)

Through the isomorphism (
4.5.4.a
), we have

\delta_{ij}=(\bar{\nu}_{i}\times_{S}\bar{\nu}_{j})^{*}\big([\Delta_{X/S}]^{2}_{X\times X}\big)

where the right-hand side is the
second
twisted fundamental class of
the diagonal of
X/S
(see
2.3.6
).

(2)

If
i=j
,
\nu^{\prime}_{ii}
is the diagonal
\delta_{i}
of
Z_{i}/S
.
We consider the map

H^{0}_{\T}(X,N_{i})\xrightarrow{\epsilon_{2*}}H^{0}_{\T}(Z_{i},N_{\delta_{i}}+\delta_{i}^{-1}p_{2}^{-1}T_{X/S})\xrightarrow{\delta_{i!}}H^{0}_{\T}(Z_{i}\times_{S}Z_{i},p_{2}^{-1}T_{X/S})

where the first map is induced by the canonical isomorphism of virtual bundles

\epsilon_{2}:\langle N_{i}\rangle\simeq\langle N_{i}\rangle-\langle T_{X/S}^{i}\rangle+\langle\delta_{i}^{-1}p_{2}^{-1}T_{X/S}\rangle\simeq\langle N_{\delta_{i}}\rangle+\langle\delta_{i}^{-1}p_{2}^{-1}T_{X/S}\rangle

over
Z_{i}
and
\delta_{i!}
is the Gysin map in cohomotopy (see
2.3.1
).
Let also
e(N_{i})
be the Euler class of the normal bundle
N_{i}
of
Z_{i}/X
(see
2.3.3
).
Then through the isomorphism (
4.5.4.a
), we have

\delta_{ii}=\delta_{i!}(\epsilon_{2*}e(N_{i}))

(3)

Assume furthermore that (
4.5.5.a
) is transversal:

\nu^{\prime}_{ij}
is regular with normal bundle isomorphic to the restriction of
T_{X}
to
Z_{ij}
,
i.e.,
it is of proper codimension.
Then
\delta_{ij}
can be computed through the isomorphism (
4.5.4.a
) as

\delta_{ij}=[Z^{\prime}_{ij}]_{Z_{i}\times Z_{j}}^{2}

Here,

[Z^{\prime}_{ij}]_{Z_{i}\times Z_{j}}^{2}\in H_{\T}^{0}(Z_{i}\times_{S}Z_{j},p_{2}^{-1}\langle T_{X}^{j}\rangle)
is the twisted fundamental class
of
\nu^{\prime}_{ij}
with respect to the obvious
\nu^{\prime}_{ij}
-parallelization.

Proof.

The first statement follows from the definition of the explicit duality pairing given in
2.5.7
,
and the properties of fundamental classes.
For compatibility with composition and transversal base change formula for closed immersions,
see
[
35
, Lemma 3.2.13, Ex. 3.2.9(i)]
.
The second (resp. third) computation follows from the first one and the excess intersection (resp. transversal base change) formula for the above cartesian square.
∎

Example 4.5.6
.

When
\T
is an oriented motivic category,
i.e.,
one of the categories under
\DM
in (
1.2.0.a
),
and we assume that the second condition of the proposition holds,
then
\delta_{ij}=[Z^{\prime}_{ij}]_{Z_{i}\times Z_{j}}
is the image of the usual cycle class of the natural
diagonal immersion of
Z^{\prime}_{ij}
by the cycle class map

\CH^{d}(Z_{i}\times_{S}Z_{j})\rightarrow H_{\T}^{2d,d}(Z_{i}\times_{S}Z_{j})

where
d
is the dimension of
X/S
.
In particular, we get
\delta_{ij}=\delta_{ji}
after making the identification

\CH^{d}(Z_{i}\times_{S}Z_{j})=\CH^{d}(Z_{j}\times_{S}Z_{i})
.
That is, the matrix in
4.2.1
is
symmetric
.
In the non-oriented case, this will no longer be true in general,
as we will illustrate in the forthcoming section.

5.
Motivic plumbing

In this section, we explain in which sense our punctured tubular neighborhood gives rise to a motivic version of Mumford’s plumbing construction from
[
71
]
and show how to extend some of the computations of
loc. cit.
in the
\mathbf{A}^{1}
-homotopical context.

5.1.
Assumptions and notation

We impose the following assumptions.

(M1)

(X,D)
is a closed
S
-pair,
S
an arbitrary base
scheme.
9
9

9

On the stable homotopy case we will often assume that
S
is semi-local.
In our examples
S
will be a field.

(M2)

X/S
has relative dimension
2
and is smooth in a neighborhood of
D
.

(M3)

D
is a divisor on
X
, proper and with smooth reduced crossings over

S
.
10
10

10

See
3.3.2
.

(M4)

The components
(D_{i})_{i\in I}
of
D
are rational curves.
For
i\in I
, we fix an isomorphism
\alpha_{i}:D_{i}\rightarrow\mathbf{P}^{1}_{S}
.
This determines
3
distinct rational points on
D_{i}
, say
0_{i},1_{i},\infty_{i}
.
We assume that the point
\infty_{i}
on
D_{i}
is not
a point on another irreducible component
D_{j}
of
D
. We let
\omega_{i}=\det(\Omega_{D_{i}/S})\cong\alpha_{i}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbf{P}^{1}_{S}}(-2)
be the canonical sheaf of
D_{i}
and we denote by
T_{i}
its associated line bundle.

Later on,
we will add one of two other assumptions (M5a)/(M5b) to this list (see
5.2.5
).
In the above situation,
we follow the conventions of the previous section:

•

Let
\Omega_{X/S}|_{D}
be the restriction to
D
of the relative cotangent sheaf of
X/S
(computed in a neighborhood of
D
) and denote by
T_{X}|_{D}
the corresponding vector bundle of rank
2
on
D
. We let
\omega_{X}|_{D}=\det(\Omega_{X/S}|_{D})
.

•

For every
i\in I
, we denote by
\nu_{i}:D_{i}\rightarrow D
and by
\bar{\nu}_{i}:D_{i}\rightarrow X
the natural closed immersions and by
p_{i}:D_{i}\to S
the projection.
We let
\mathcal{C}_{i}=\mathcal{C}_{D_{i}/X}
be the conormal sheaf of
D_{i}
in
X
, and we denote by
N_{i}
the associated line bundle.

•

We fix an arbitrary order on
I
. For
i<j
in
I
, we put
D^{\prime}_{ij}=D_{i}\times_{X}D_{j}
, and let
D_{ij}
be its reduction. Under our assumptions,
the projection
p_{ij}:D_{ij}\rightarrow S
is a finite étale morphism.
There are closed immersions
\nu_{ij}^{l}:D_{ij}\rightarrow D_{l}
,
l=i,j
. We set
\mathcal{C}_{ij}=\mathcal{C}_{D_{ij}/X}
and
\mathcal{C}_{ij}^{l}=\mathcal{C}_{D_{ij}/D_{l}}
the conormal sheaves of
D_{ij}
in
X
and
D_{l}
respectively,
l=i,j
, and we denote by
N_{ij}
and
N_{ij}^{l}
the associated vector bundles of respective rank
2
and
1
on
D_{ij}
.

For
i<j
, there are canonical isomorphisms

(5.1.0.a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{ij}

\displaystyle\simeq\Omega_{X}|_{D_{ij}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{ij}^{l}

\displaystyle\simeq\omega_{l}|_{D_{ij}},\quad l=i,j

(5.1.0.b)

\displaystyle\omega_{X}|_{D_{i}}

\displaystyle\simeq\mathcal{C}_{i}\ \otimes\omega_{i},

\displaystyle\det(\mathcal{C}_{ij})

\displaystyle\simeq\mathcal{C}_{ij}^{l}\otimes\mathcal{C}_{l}|_{D_{ij}}\quad l=i,j

These define canonical isomorphisms of virtual vector bundles
T_{X}|_{D_{i}}\simeq T_{i}+N_{i}
and

T_{X}|_{D_{ij}}\simeq N_{l}|_{D_{ij}}+N_{ij}^{l}\simeq N_{l}|_{D_{ij}}+T_{l}|_{D_{ij}}\quad l=i,j

To get more precise results, we will also introduce at some point (see (
5.2.8
and the subsequent results) the following strengthening of assumption (M3):

(M3+)

S
is the spectrum of a field
k
,
the irreducible components
D_{i}
of
D
intersect transversely (at all points
D_{ij}^{\lambda}
),
and the residue field
\kappa_{ij}^{\lambda}
of
D_{ij}^{\lambda}
is finite separable over
k
.

The transversality assumption implies that for every
i<j
, we have
D_{ij}=D_{i}\times_{X}D_{j}=\bigsqcup_{\lambda}D_{ij}^{\lambda}
. Furthermore, it guarantees for all
i\neq j
and all
\lambda
the existence of a canonical isomorphism
\mathcal{C}_{i}|_{D_{ij}^{\lambda}}\simeq\mathcal{C}_{ij}^{j}|_{D_{ij}^{\lambda}}
and
hence of a canonical isomorphism

(5.1.0.c)

\mathcal{C}_{i}|_{D_{ij}^{\lambda}}\otimes\omega_{j}^{\vee}|_{D_{ij}^{\lambda}}\simeq\mathcal{C}_{ij}^{j}|_{D_{ij}^{\lambda}}\otimes\omega_{j}^{\vee}|_{D_{ij}^{\lambda}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:Mumford_vb1}}}{{\simeq}}\omega_{j}|_{D_{ij}^{\lambda}}\otimes\omega_{j}^{\vee}|_{D_{ij}^{\lambda}}\simeq\kappa_{ij}^{\lambda}

5.2.
Punctured tubular neighborhoods and quadratic Mumford matrices

Under a reasonable orientability assumption,
the computations in this subsection apply to any motivic
\infty
-category
\T
.
We will later focus on the (universal) cases of
\SH
and
\DM_{\mathbb{Q}}
.

5.2.1
.

Under the assumptions and notation in
Section

5.1
,
we will use the formula given in
4.2.1
in two steps.
First, since
D
is an h-smooth crossing
S
-scheme,

3.3.3
identifies
\Pi_{S}(D)
with the homotopy cofiber of the canonical map

(5.2.1.a)

d_{1}=\sum_{i<j}\nu^{i}_{ij*}-\nu^{j}_{ij*}:\bigoplus_{i<j}\Pi_{S}(D_{ij})\rightarrow\bigoplus_{i\in I}\Pi_{S}(D_{i})

The isomorphisms
\alpha_{i}
of
5.1
(M4) determine an isomorphism

(5.2.1.b)

\gamma_{1}=\sum_{i\in I}\alpha_{i*}:\bigoplus_{i\in I}\Pi_{S}(D_{i})\rightarrow\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathbf{1}_{S}\oplus\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathbf{1}_{S}(1)[2]

The following lemma is immediate.

Lemma 5.2.2
.

Under the above assumptions and notation,
the composite
\gamma_{1}\circ d_{1}
factors through the inclusion of the direct summand
\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathbf{1}_{S}

on the right-hand-side of (
5.2.1.b
).
Moreover,
the factorization coincides with the map

(5.2.2.a)

q_{1}=\sum_{i<j}p_{ij*}^{i}-p_{ij*}^{j}:\bigoplus_{i<j}\Pi_{S}(D_{ij})\rightarrow\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathbf{1}_{S}

where
p_{ij*}^{l}
is the composition of the inclusion of the
l
-factor of the left hand-side with
p_{ij*}
.

In particular, there is a canonical isomorphism

(5.2.2.b)

\Pi_{S}(D)\simeq\mathcal{D}\oplus\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathbf{1}_{S}(1)[2]

where
\mathcal{D}
is the homotopy cofiber of
q_{1}
.

Remark 5.2.3
.

One can interpret the preceding lemma by saying that
\Pi_{S}(D)
is a sum of the combinatorial
part
\mathcal{D}
depending on the combinatorics of the intersection of the irreducible components of
D
,
which is a smooth Artin object
11
11

11

By analogy with the case of motives,
it is the smallest
\infty
-category containing
\Pi_{S}(V)
for
V/S
finite étale,
and stable under suspensions, homotopy (co)fibers.

and the “geometric” part
\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathbf{1}_{S}(1)[2]
.

5.2.4
.

The description of the target
\Pi_{S}(X/X-D)
of the map
\beta_{X,D}
in
4.1.1
is more involved, in particular in the non-oriented case.

Lemma 5.2.5
.

Consider the assumptions of
Section

5.1
.
Assume one of the following condition holds:

(M5a)

The motivic
\infty
-category
\T
is oriented.

(M5b)

The motivic category is
\T=\tDM
, or
\T=\SH
and
K_{0}(S)
is infinite cyclic.
Furthermore, we assume given for every
i\in I
two orientation classes

\epsilon_{i}\in\mathscr{O}r_{D_{i}}(\mathcal{C}_{i})
and
\tau_{i}\in\mathscr{O}r_{D_{i}}(\omega_{i})
(see
6.1.5
) such that for every
i<j
, the equality

(\epsilon_{i}\otimes\tau_{i})|_{D_{ij}}=(\epsilon_{j}\otimes\tau_{j})|_{D_{ij}}

holds in
\mathscr{O}r_{D_{ij}}(\omega_{X}|_{D_{ij}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:Mumford_vb2}}}{{\simeq}}\mathscr{O}r_{D_{ij}}((\mathcal{C}_{i}\otimes\omega_{i})|_{D_{ij}})
.

Then there exists an isomorphism

\epsilon_{*}:\Pi_{S}(X/X-D)\rightarrow\Pi_{S}(D)^{\vee}(2)[4]=\uHom(\Pi_{S}(D),\mathbf{1}_{S})(2)[4]

in
\T(S)
, canonical in case (M5a) and depending canonically on the orientations classes
(\epsilon_{i})_{i\in I}
and
(\tau_{i})_{i\in I}
in case (M5b).

Combining the isomorphism
\epsilon_{*}
with
5.2.2
, we get the isomorphism

(5.2.5.a)

\Pi_{S}(X/X-D)\simeq\mathcal{D}^{\vee}(2)[4]\oplus\bigoplus_{j\in I}\mathbf{1}_{S}(1)[2]

Note moreover that
\mathcal{D}^{\vee}
is still an Artin object as in
5.2.3
.

Remark 5.2.6
.

(1)

Assumption (M5b) implies that the conormal sheaves
\mathcal{C}_{i}
of all the divisors
D_{i}
in
X
must have even degree.

(2)

When
\omega_{X}|_{D}
is orientable, say with a given orientation class
\varphi\in\mathscr{O}r_{D}(\omega_{X}|_{D})
, one way of fulfilling condition (M5b) is to choose for
\tau_{i}
the
orientation given by the canonical isomorphism
c_{i}:\omega_{i}\to(\mathcal{O}_{D_{i}}(-1))^{\otimes 2}

and for
\epsilon_{i}
the image of the orientation class

\varphi|_{D_{i}}\otimes\,\tau_{i}^{\vee}\in\mathscr{O}r_{D_{i}}(\omega_{X}|_{D_{i}}\otimes\omega_{i}^{\vee})

under the isomorphism
\mathscr{O}r_{D_{i}}(\omega_{X}|_{D_{i}}\otimes\omega_{i}^{\vee})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:Mumford_vb2}}}{{\simeq}}\mathscr{O}r_{D_{i}}(\mathcal{C}_{i})
.

Proof.

In the case of assumption (M5a),
the lemma follows from
3.4.3
by canonically trivializing twists of vector bundles.

Next we treat the more involved case of (M5b).
Consider the Gysin morphisms

\displaystyle(\nu_{ij}^{l})^{!}:\Pi_{S}(D_{l},-\langle T_{l}\rangle)(2)[4]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:Mumford_vb1}}}{{\simeq}}\Pi_{S}(D_{l},-\langle N_{ij}^{l}\rangle)(2)[4]\rightarrow\Pi_{S}(D_{ij})(2)[4]

\displaystyle(\nu_{ij}^{l})^{!}:\Pi_{S}(D_{l},N_{l})\rightarrow\Pi_{S}(D_{ij},(\nu_{ij}^{l})^{-1}N_{l}-\langle N_{ij}^{l}\rangle)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:Mumford_vb2}}}{{\simeq}}\Pi_{S}(D_{ij},N_{ij})

Then for every
l=i,j
, we have a commutative diagram of isomorphisms

\textstyle{\Pi_{S}(D_{l})^{\vee}(2)[4]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\nu^{l}_{ij*})^{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{l}}
\textstyle{\Pi_{S}(D_{ij})^{\vee}(2)[4]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{ij}}
\textstyle{\Pi_{S}(D_{l},-\langle T_{l}\rangle)(2)[4]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\nu^{l}_{ij})^{!}}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{*}^{(l)}}
\textstyle{\Pi_{S}(D_{ij})(2)[4]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon_{*}^{(ij,l)}}
\textstyle{\Pi_{S}(D_{l},N_{l})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\nu^{l}_{ij})^{!}}
\textstyle{\Pi_{S}(D_{ij},N_{ij})}

The top square is the canonical commutative diagram coming from the explicit duality pairing constructed in
2.5.7
. The vertical isomorphism of the bottom square is defined as follows:
The isomorphism
\epsilon_{*}^{(ij,l)}
is obtained either from the
\mathbf{SL}
-orientation of
\tDM
or in case
\T=\SH
from
6.1.16
by using the orientation
(\epsilon_{l}\otimes\tau_{l})|_{D_{ij}}
of
\det\mathcal{C}_{ij}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:Mumford_vb1}}}{{\simeq}}\omega_{X}|_{D_{ij}}
. The isomorphism
\eta_{*}^{(l)}
is obtained in the same way by using the isomorphisms

\Pi_{S}(D_{l},N_{l})\simeq\Pi_{S}(D_{l})(1)[2]\quad\textrm{and}\quad\Pi_{S}(D_{l},-\langle T_{l}\rangle)\simeq\Pi_{S}(D_{l})(-1)[-2]

associated to the chosen orientations

\epsilon_{l}
and
\tau_{l}
, respectively.
The assumption that the orientations
(\epsilon_{i}\otimes\tau_{i})|_{D_{ij}}
and
(\epsilon_{j}\otimes\tau_{j})|_{D_{ij}}
coincide in
\mathscr{O}r_{D_{ij}}(\omega_{X}|_{D_{ij}})

ensures that
\epsilon_{*}^{(ij,i)}=\epsilon_{*}^{(ij,j)}
. It follows that the diagram

\textstyle{\bigoplus_{i\in I}\Pi_{S}(D_{i})^{\vee}(2)[4]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sum_{i<j}(\nu^{i}_{ij*})^{\vee}-(\nu^{j}_{ij*})^{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{\bigoplus\eta_{*}^{(i)}\theta_{i}}
\textstyle{\bigoplus_{i<j}\Pi_{S}(D_{ij})^{\vee}(2)[4]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bigoplus\epsilon_{*}^{(ij,i)}\theta_{ij}=\bigoplus\epsilon_{*}^{(ij,j)}\theta_{ij}}
\textstyle{\bigoplus_{i\in I}\Pi_{S}(D_{l},N_{l})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sum_{i<j}(\nu^{i}_{ij})^{!}-(\nu^{j}_{ij})^{!}}
\textstyle{\bigoplus_{i<j}\Pi_{S}(D_{ij},N_{ij})}

is commutative. This provides the desired canonical isomorphism between the homotopy fiber
\Pi_{S}(D)^{\vee}(2)[4]

of top line (see (
5.2.1.a
)) and the homotopy fiber
\Pi_{S}(X/X-D)
of the bottom line (see
3.3.10
).
∎

Using the above two lemmas, we can refine
4.2.1
as follows:

Theorem 5.2.7
.

Assume conditions (M1)-(M4) of
Section

5.1

as well as one of the conditions (M5a) or (M5b) of
5.2.5
.
Then the punctured tubular neighborhood
\operatorname{TN}^{\times}_{S}(X,D)
in
\T(S)
,
or equivalently (
4.4.2
) the homotopy at infinity
\Pi^{\infty}_{S}(X-D)

when
X/S
is in addition
proper
, is isomorphic to the homotopy fiber of the map

\beta=\begin{pmatrix}a&b^{\prime}\\
b&\mu\end{pmatrix}:\mathcal{D}\oplus\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathbf{1}_{S}(1)[2]\rightarrow\mathcal{D}^{\vee}(2)[4]\oplus\bigoplus_{j\in I}\mathbf{1}_{S}(1)[2]

Here
\mu:\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathbf{1}_{S}(1)[2]\rightarrow\bigoplus_{j\in I}\mathbf{1}_{S}(1)[2]

is given by a square matrix
(\mu_{ij})_{i,j\in I^{2}}
with coefficients in the endomorphism ring

\End_{\T}(\mathbf{1}_{S})
.

In the case (M5b), we call
\mu
the
quadratic Mumford matrix
of
(X,D)
. The next proposition provides an explicit description of this matrix.

Proposition 5.2.8
.

We assume conditions (M1)-(M4) of
Section

5.1
.

•

In case (M5a), and
\T=\DM,\DM_{\textrm{\'{e}t}},\DB,D(-_{\textrm{\'{e}t}},\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}),\DHdg
(see diagram (
1.2.0.a
)) we have
\End_{\T}(k)=\mathbb{Z}
and for every
i,j
,

\mu_{ij}=\deg([D_{i}]\cdot[D_{j}])=(D_{i},D_{j})

is the usual
intersection number
of the (effective Cartier) divisors
D_{i}
and
D_{j}
.

•

In case (M5b),

\End_{\T}(\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{Z}[\frac{1}{2}]})\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{(2)}=\GW(\mathbb{Z}[\frac{1}{2}])\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{(2)}
12
12

12

See
[
16
, §5]
for more general results.

and
\End_{\T}(\mathbf{1}_{k})=\GW(k)
for any field
k
.
Then,
for every
i
,
we have

\mu_{ii}=\tdeg_{\tau^{\prime}_{i}}e(N_{i},\epsilon_{i})

where
e(N_{i},\epsilon_{i})\in\widetilde{\mathrm{CH}}{}^{1}(D_{i})

is the Euler class of the oriented bundle
(N_{i},\epsilon_{i})
,
and
\tdeg_{\tau^{\prime}_{i}}:\widetilde{\mathrm{CH}}{}^{1}(X)\rightarrow\GW(k)
is the quadratic
\tau^{\prime}_{i}
-degree
associated with the quadratic isomorphism
\tau^{\prime}_{i}=\tau_{i}^{-1}:\mathcal{O}_{D_{i}}\rightarrowtail\omega_{i}

(see (

) and Remark

(2)).

Furthermore, under the additional assumption (M3+) and for every
i\neq j
, we have

\mu_{ij}=\sum_{\lambda}\langle\Tr_{\kappa_{ij}^{\lambda}/k}(u_{ij}^{\lambda}\cdot xy)\rangle

Here
\Tr_{\kappa_{ij}^{\lambda}/k}
is the trace form (in variables
x,y
),
and
u_{ij}^{\lambda}\in\kappa_{ij}^{\lambda}
is a unit whose quadratic class is the image of the orientation class

\epsilon_{ij}^{\lambda}=(\epsilon_{i}\otimes\tau_{j}^{\vee})|_{D_{ij}^{\lambda}}\in\mathscr{O}r_{D_{ij}^{\lambda}}((\mathcal{C}_{i}\otimes\omega_{j}^{\vee})|_{D_{ij}^{\lambda}})

under the isomorphism

\mathscr{O}r_{D_{ij}^{\lambda}}((\mathcal{C}_{i}\otimes\omega_{j}^{\vee})|_{D_{ij}^{\lambda}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:Mumford_vb3}}}{{\simeq}}\mathscr{O}r_{D_{ij}^{\lambda}}(\kappa_{ij}^{\lambda})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{rem:compare_or}}}{{\simeq}}Q(\kappa^{\lambda}_{ij})
.

Proof.

By construction, for every
(i,j)\in I^{2}
, the coefficient
\mu_{ij}
is computed
as the composite map

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{S}(1)[2]\xrightarrow{p_{i}^{!}}\Pi_{S}(D_{i},-T_{i})(1)[2]\xrightarrow{(1)}\Pi_{S}(D_{i})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{(\bar{\nu}_{i})_{*}}\Pi_{S}(X)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{(\bar{\nu}_{j})^{!}}\Pi_{S}(D_{j},N_{j})\xrightarrow{(2)}\Pi_{S}(D_{j})(1)[2]\xrightarrow{(p_{j})_{*}}\mathbf{1}_{S}(1)[2]

Here
p_{i}:D_{i}\rightarrow\Spec(k)
(resp.
\bar{\nu}_{i}:D_{i}\rightarrow X
) is the projection map (resp. inclusion),
the map (1) (resp. (2)) is the isomorphism from
5.2.2
(
5.2.5
).
The case (M5a) follows readily. In the case (M5b), (1) corresponds to the isomorphism induced by the orientation
\tau_{i}

and (2) from the orientation
\epsilon_{j}
.
The assertion then follows readily from the definitions.
∎

Remark 5.2.9
.

(1)

The assumption made in

of

(2)

The element

coincides with the Euler number

of the zero section

of

with respect to the

(see

for explanations)
of

considered by Bachmann-Wickelgren in

]
.
One can check that in our setting, this element is actually independent on the chosen orientations, equal to
\frac{1}{2}(D_{i},D_{i})h
, where

is the class of the hyperbolic plane and where
(D_{i},D_{i})=\deg(\mathcal{C}_{i}^{\vee})\in 2\mathbb{Z}
is the usual self-intersection number of

D_{i}
13
13

13

\mathcal{C}_{i}
has even degree on account of being orientable, see

(1).
.
In contrast,

,

and

.

5.3.
Abelian mixed Artin-Tate motives

5.3.1
.

In the following, we apply
5.2.7
to rational abelian mixed Artin-Tate motives.
That is,
we use
\T=\DM_{\mathbb{Q}}
, and restrict for simplicity to the case
S=\Spec(K)
for some field
K
.

Let
(X,D)
be a
K
-pair satisfying the assumptions (M1)-(M4).

5.2.7
implies that the motive
M(\operatorname{TN}^{\times}(X,D))
over
K
is Artin-Tate:
it is in the smallest thick triangulated subcategory
\DMAT(K,\mathbb{Q})
of
\DM(K,\mathbb{Q})
which contains motives
of the form
M(L)(n)
,
where
L/K
is a finite separable extension of
K
.

To state the next result,
we moreover consider one of the following settings:

(1)

Assume
K
is a field of Kronecker index at most
one;
14
14

14

Recall the Kronecker index of a field
F
,
of transcendence degree
d
over its prime subfield and characteristic
p
,
is either
d+1
if
p=0
or
d
if
p>0
.

for example, a number field,
a finite field or a finitely generated field of transcendence degree
1
over a finite field.
We let
\DMAT(K,\mathbb{Q})
be the triangulated category of (constructible) Artin-Tate motives over
\mathbb{Q}
.
From
[
65
]
it follows that
\DMAT(K,\mathbb{Q})
admits a motivic t-structure (uniquely characterized),
whose heart is the Tannakian category
\MMAT(K,\mathbb{Q})
of abelian Artin-Tate motives.
In particular,
one gets a homological and monoidal functor

\HM_{0}:\DMAT(K,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\MMAT(K,\mathbb{Q})

(2)

Assume
K
is a field of characteristic
0
with a fixed complex embedding.
Then we can consider the Tannakian category
\MMN(K)
of Nori motives over
K
,
as defined in
[
55
]
,
together with its canonical (universal) homological functor

\HM_{0}:\DM_{gm}(K,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\MMN(K)

In that case, we define the category of Artin-Tate-Nori motives
\MMAT(K,\mathbb{Q})
as the smallest thick abelian
subcategory of
\MMN(K)
which contains
\HM_{0}\DMAT(K,\mathbb{Q})
. As in the previous case, we get a homological functor.

Under these assumptions,
we define the Artin-Tate-Nori motive

\HM_{i}(\operatorname{TN}^{\times}(X,D)):=\HM_{0}(\operatorname{TN}^{\times}(X,D)[-i])

as the
i
-th (motivic) homology of the punctured tubular neighborhood of
(X,D)
.
When
X
is in addition proper over
K
,
this is the homology of the boundary motive of
(X-D)

(see
4.3.3
and
4.4.2
),
or the
motivic homology at infinity

\HM_{i}^{\infty}(X-D)=\HM_{i}(\operatorname{TN}^{\times}(X,D))

Proposition 5.3.2
.

Under the above assumptions,
the homology motive
\HM_{i}(X)
vanishes for
i\not\in[0,3]
and there is an exact sequence

\displaystyle 0\rightarrow

\displaystyle\HM_{3}(\operatorname{TN}^{\times}(X,D))\rightarrow\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathbf{1}_{S}(2)\xrightarrow{\sum_{i<j}p_{ij}^{i!}-p_{ij}^{j!}}\bigoplus_{i<j}M_{S}(D_{ij})(2)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\HM_{2}(\operatorname{TN}^{\times}(X,D))\rightarrow\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathbf{1}_{S}(1)\xrightarrow{\ \mu\ }\bigoplus_{j\in I}\mathbf{1}_{S}(1)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\HM_{1}(\operatorname{TN}^{\times}(X,D))\rightarrow\bigoplus_{i<j}M_{S}(D_{ij})\xrightarrow{\sum_{i<j}p^{i}_{ij*}-p^{j}_{ij*}}\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathbf{1}_{S}\rightarrow\HM_{0}(\operatorname{TN}^{\times}(X,D))\rightarrow 0

Here,

\mu
is the Mumford matrix,
and
M_{S}(D_{ij})=\HM_{0}(M_{S}(D_{ij}))
is seen as an abelian Artin-Tate motive,
or as an Artin-Tate-Nori motive.

Note in particular that
\HM_{0}(\operatorname{TN}^{\times}(X,D))
and
\HM_{3}(\operatorname{TN}^{\times}(X,D))
are pure of respective weights
0
and
-4
,
while
\HM_{1}(\operatorname{TN}^{\times}(X,D))
and
\HM_{2}(\operatorname{TN}^{\times}(X,D))
are in general mixed of weights
\{0,-2\}
and
\{-2,-4\}
,
respectively (see
[
58
]
for the notion of weights on Artin-Tate-Nori motives).

Proof.

5.2.7
provides a distinguished triangle computing
M(\operatorname{TN}^{\times}(X,D))
.
The above long exact sequence follows by using the long exact sequence associated with the homological functor
\HM_{0}
.
∎

Remark 5.3.3
.

One obtains similar exact sequences of mixed motives over more general bases
S
using:

(1)

[
80
]
: when
S\subset\Spec{\mathcal{O}_{K}}
,
\mathcal{O}_{K}
a number ring;

(2)

[
58
]
:
S
a smooth
K
-scheme, for a field
K
with a complex embedding
K\subset\mathbb{C}
.

Indeed,
the indicated references provide us with a suitable category of Artin-Tate(-Nori) motives,
and one can make precisely the same calculation
(taking into account the dimension of
S
as we use perverse motivic t-structures).

Example 5.3.4
.

To illustrate
5.2.7
,
5.2.8
,
we compute Wildeshaus’
boundary motive
,
or equivalently the motive at infinity (
4.3.3
),
of
Ramanujam’s surface

\Sigma
over a field
k
of characteristic different from
2
.
We work in
\T=\DM
, the integral category of motives.

First we recall the construction of
\Sigma
.
Given a cuspidal cubic
C\subset\mathbf{P}^{2}_{k}
and a smooth
k
-rational conic
Q\subset\mathbf{P}^{2}_{k}

intersecting
C
with multiplicity
5
in a
k
-rational point
p
,
let
\Sigma
be the complement of the proper transforms of
C
and
Q
in the blow-up

\sigma:\mathbf{F}_{1}\to\mathbf{P}^{2}_{k}
of the remaining
k
-rational intersection point
q
of
C
and
Q

(see
[
51
]
for Hirzebruch surfaces
\mathbf{F}_{n}
,
n\geq 0
).
Over the complex numbers,
the underlying analytic space of
\Sigma
is a topologically contractible open smooth manifold non-homeomorphic
to
{\bf R}^{4}
whose topological fundamental group at infinity
\pi^{\infty}_{1}(\Sigma)
is infinite
with trivial abelianization,
see
[
74
]
.

A compactification
X=\bar{\Sigma}
of
\Sigma
with smooth crossing boundary
D=\partial\Sigma
is obtained from
\mathbf{F}_{1}

by blowing-up the singular point of
C
,
with exceptional divisor
E\simeq\mathbf{P}^{1}_{k}
.
The irreducible components of
D
are then
E
and the proper transforms of
Q
and
C
,
with respective self-intersections
E^{2}=-1
,
Q^{2}=4
and
C^{2}=3
.
Furthermore,
Q
and
C
intersects with multiplicity
5
at the unique point
p
and
E
and
Q
intersects
with multiplicity
2
at a unique
k
-rational point.

Next we apply
5.2.7
to the pair
(X,D)
.
One first obtains that the
Artin part

\mathcal{D}=\mathbf{1}_{k}
, and that the maps
a
,
b
,
b^{\prime}

are all zero for degree reasons (see also the proof of
5.4.2
).
Then from
5.2.8
,
the map
\mu:(\mathbf{1}_{k}(1)[2])^{\oplus 3}\to(\mathbf{1}_{k}(1)[2])^{\oplus 3}
is given by the integer valued intersection matrix

\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}4&5&2\\
5&3&0\\
2&0&-1\end{array}\right)

Its Smith normal form is the diagonal matrix
\Delta(1,1,1)
in
\mathrm{M}_{3,3}(\mathbb{Z})
.

5.2.7
implies the boundary motive of
\Sigma
is isomorphic to homotopy fiber
of the trivial map
\mathbf{1}_{k}\to\mathbf{1}_{k}(2)[4]
.
In summary, we obtain

\partial M(\Sigma)=M^{\infty}(\Sigma)\simeq\mathbf{1}_{k}\oplus\mathbf{1}_{k}(2)[3]

5.4.
Punctured tubular neighborhoods of orientable trees of rational curves

5.4.1
.

Consider the assumptions (M1)-(M4), (M3+) of
Section

5.1
, in the special case where
D
is an orientable tree of smooth
k
-rational curves on a smooth surface
X/k
over a field
k
, that is:

(1)

D
is a smooth normal crossing divisor on
X
with irreducible components
D_{i}\simeq\mathbb{P}^{1}_{k}
,
i\in I
, such that for every
i\neq j
,
D_{ij}
is either empty or consists of a single
k
-rational point.

(2)

For every
i\in I
, the conormal sheaf
\mathcal{C}_{i}
of
D_{i}
is
X
is orientable, hence isomorphic to
\mathcal{O}_{D_{i}}(2n_{i})
for some
n_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(3)

The incidence complex
\Gamma
of
D
is a tree.

Recall that
h=\langle 1\rangle+\langle-1\rangle=1+\langle-1\rangle\in\GW(k)
denotes the class of the hyperbolic plane.
As an application of the general computation of
5.2.8
, we get:

Proposition 5.4.2
.

Under the above assumptions, there is a choice of orientations
(\epsilon_{i})_{i\in I}

fulfilling condition (M5b) of
5.2.5

which guarantees that the punctured tubular neighborhood
\operatorname{TN}^{\times}_{S}(X,D)
in
\SH(k)
is isomorphic to

\mathbf{1}_{k}\oplus\mathrm{hofib}(\mathbf{\mu})\oplus\mathbf{1}_{k}(2)[3]

where the quadratic Mumford matrix
\mu:\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathbf{1}_{k}(1)[2]\to\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathbf{1}_{k}(1)[2]

is obtained from the integer-valued Mumford matrix
(D_{i},D_{j})_{i,j}
by replacing each
diagonal entry
(D_{i},D_{i})=-2n_{i}
by
-n_{i}h
.

Proof.

First, assume any choice of orientation
\epsilon_{i}
fulfilling condition (M5b).
Denote by
J\subset I\times I
the subset consisting of pairs
i<j

such that
D_{ij}\neq\emptyset
. Since
\Gamma
is a tree,
we have
\sharp J=\sharp I-1
and
\bigoplus_{i<j}\Pi_{k}(D_{ij})=\bigoplus_{(i,j)\in J}\mathbf{1}_{k}
. The
map
q_{1}
in (
5.2.2.a
)
is given by a matrix in
\mathrm{M}_{\sharp J,\sharp I}(\mathbb{Z})
,
whose Smith normal form is the diagonal matrix

\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathrm{id}_{\sharp J}\\
0\end{array}\right)

The homotopy cofiber
\mathcal{D}
of
q_{1}
is thus equal to that of the trivial map
0\to\mathbf{1}_{S}
, hence to
\mathbf{1}_{S}
.
This implies that
\mathcal{D}^{\vee}=\mathbf{1}_{S}
.
By Morel’s
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-connectivity theorem,
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{SH}(k)}(\mathbf{1}_{k},\mathbf{1}_{k}(i)[2i])=0
for all
i>0
.
Thus
5.2.7
implies that
\operatorname{TN}^{\times}_{S}(X,D)
is the homotopy fiber of the map

\beta=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&\mu\end{pmatrix}:\mathbf{1}_{S}\oplus\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathbf{1}_{S}(1)[2]\rightarrow\mathcal{\mathbf{1}}_{S}(2)[4]\oplus\bigoplus_{j\in I}\mathbf{1}_{S}(1)[2]

By
5.2.8
and
5.2.9
(2), the diagonal entries of
\mu
are equal to the Euler classes
e(\mathcal{C}_{i}^{\vee})=e(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}_{k}}(-2n_{i}))=-n_{i}h\in\GW(k)
.

To finish the proof, we will now show that, up to modifying the
\tau_{i}
,
there always exists a choice of orientations
\epsilon_{i}
fulfilling condition (M5b)

and
such that the orientations
\epsilon_{ij}
of
\kappa(D_{ij})=k
appearing in
5.2.8
are all equivalent to the canonical orientation of
k
defined as the inverse of the multiplication homomorphism
m:k\otimes k\to k
,
a\otimes b\mapsto ab
. In turn, this shows our remaining assertion about the coefficients away from the diagonal in the matrix
\mu
.

This can be seen as follows. Let
D_{0}
be any irreducible component of
D
, which we view as the root of the incidence tree
\Gamma
of
D
and denote by
D_{1},\ldots D_{s}
be the irreducible components of
D
which interest
D_{0}
. Let
\epsilon_{0}:\mathcal{C}_{0}\to\mathcal{L}_{0}^{\otimes 2}
and
\tau_{0}:\omega_{0}\to\mathcal{M}_{0}^{\otimes 2}
be any fixed choice of orientations. For any orientation
\tau_{j}:\omega_{j}\to\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\otimes 2}
,
j=1,\ldots,s

the orientation

\epsilon_{j,0}=\epsilon_{j}\otimes\,\!^{t}\tau_{0}^{-1}:k\simeq\mathcal{C}_{j}|_{D_{0,j}}\otimes\omega_{0}^{\vee}|_{D_{0,j}}\to(\mathcal{L}_{j}|_{D_{0,j}}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{0}^{\vee}|_{D_{0,j}})^{\otimes 2}

is equivalent to
u_{0,j}\cdot m^{-1}:k\to k\otimes k
, defined by
1\mapsto u_{0,j}1\otimes 1
for some element
u_{0,j}\in k^{*}
uniquely determined up to multiplication by an element of
(k^{*})^{2}
. If
u_{0,j}\neq 1
then by replacing
\tau_{j}
by
\tau_{j}\circ(\times u_{0,j}^{-1})
we obtain an orientation whose associated unit
u_{0,j}
of
k
as in
5.2.8
is equal to
1
.
The same argument implies the existence of orientations
\epsilon_{j}:\mathcal{C}_{j}\to\mathcal{L}_{j}^{\otimes 2}
,
j=1,\ldots s
, such that for all
j=1,\ldots s
, the unit
u_{j,0}
of
k
associated to the orientation

\epsilon_{0,j}=\epsilon_{0}\otimes\,\!^{t}\tau_{j}^{-1}:k\simeq\mathcal{C}_{0}|_{D_{0,j}}\otimes\omega_{j}^{\vee}|_{D_{0,j}}\to(\mathcal{L}_{0}|_{D_{0,j}}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\vee}|_{D_{0,j}})^{\otimes 2}

is equal to
1
.

Since the incidence complex
\Gamma
of
D
is a tree, the incidence complex of
\bigcup_{i\neq 0}D_{i}
is a union of trees
\Gamma_{j}
with the components
D_{j}
,
j=1,\ldots,s
as their respective roots, with the just constructed orientations

\tau_{j}:\omega_{j}\to\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\otimes 2}
and
\epsilon_{j}:\mathcal{C}_{j}\to\mathcal{L}_{j}^{\otimes 2}
. By repeating for each of these trees the same argument as above, we obtain by induction the existence of a collection of orientations
\tau_{i}
and
\epsilon_{i}
for which the units
u_{ij}\in k=\kappa_{ij}
associated to the orientations
\epsilon_{ij}
and
\epsilon_{ji}
are all equal to
1
.

We claim the constructed collection of orientations has the property that for every
i\neq j
, the orientations
(\epsilon_{i}\otimes\tau_{i})|_{D_{ij}}
and
(\epsilon_{j}\otimes\tau_{i})|_{D_{ij}}
of
\omega_{X}|_{D_{ij}}
appearing in
5.2.5

are equivalent. Indeed, for

o_{ij}=(\tau_{i}\otimes\tau_{j})|_{D_{ij}}:\mathcal{F}_{ij}=\omega_{i}|_{D_{ij}}\otimes\omega_{j}|_{D_{ij}}\to(\mathcal{M}_{i}|_{D_{ij}}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{j}|_{D_{ij}})^{\otimes 2}

the orientations
(\epsilon_{i}\otimes\tau_{i})|_{D_{ij}}
and
(\epsilon_{j}\otimes\tau_{i})|_{D_{ij}}

are obtained by tensoring the equivalent ones
\epsilon_{ij}
and
\epsilon_{ji}
with
o_{ij}
using the canonical isomorphisms

\mathcal{C}_{i}|_{D_{ij}}\otimes\omega_{i}|_{D_{ij}}\simeq\mathcal{C}_{i}|_{D_{ij}}\otimes\omega_{j}^{\vee}|_{D_{ij}}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{ij}\;\;\textrm{and}\;\;\mathcal{C}_{j}|_{D_{ij}}\otimes\omega_{i}|_{D_{ij}}\simeq\mathcal{C}_{j}|_{D_{ij}}\otimes\omega_{i}^{\vee}|_{D_{ij}}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{ij}

The assertion follows now from the application of the formula in
5.2.8
which gives

\mu_{ij}=\langle\Tr_{\kappa_{ij}/k}(u_{ij}\cdot xy)\rangle=\langle 1\rangle=1=(D_{i},D_{j})\in\GW(k)

∎

In the next subsection, we illustrate our techniques by explicitly computing the punctured tubular neighborhoods
of Du Val singularities on normal surfaces and the stable homotopy types at infinity of Danielewski hypersurfaces,
a family of smooth affine surfaces of historical interest in the context of the Zariski cancellation problem.

Example 1: Stable motivic links of Du Val singularities on normal surfaces

Let
X_{0}
be a geometrically integral normal surface essentially of finite type over a field
k

with an isolated
k
-rational rational double point
x
, also called a Du Val singularity.
Recall from
[
2
]
,
[
3
]
that among many equivalent characterizations,
this means that letting
\pi:X\to X_{0}
be the minimal desingularization of
X_{0}
and

\pi_{\bar{k}}:X_{\bar{k}}\to X_{0,\bar{k}}
be the base change to an algebraic closure
\bar{k}
of
k
,
the following holds:

(1)

\pi^{-1}_{\bar{k}}(x_{\bar{k}})
is a smooth normal crossing divisor whose irreducible components are proper
\bar{k}
-rational curves
E_{i}
intersecting each other transversely at
\bar{k}
-rational points only.

(2)

The curves
E_{i}
have self-intersection number
-2
and the intersection matrix
(E_{i},E_{j})_{i,j}

is negative definite.

The incidence graph of the divisor
E=\pi^{-1}_{\bar{k}}(x_{\bar{k}})
is one of the classical Dynkin diagram of type
A_{n}
,
n\geq 1
,

D_{n}
,
n\geq 4
,
E_{6}
,
E_{7}
and
E_{8}
depicted in the left column of Table
1
. If
\bar{k}
has characteristic different from
2
,
3
and
5
, the completion of the local ring
\mathcal{O}_{X_{0,\bar{k}},x_{\bar{k}}}
is isomorphic to
\bar{k}[[x,y,z]]/(f)
where
f
is one of the polynomials listed in the second column of Table
1
, in particular the analytic local isomorphism type of the singularity depends only on the Dynkin diagram.
15
15

15

In characteristics
2
,
3
and
5
, there are finitely many additional ”normal forms”, see
[
3
]
for the complete list.
Over a non-closed field, Du Val singularities
A_{n}
,
D_{n}
and
E_{6}
can in general have non-trivial
k
-forms depending on the action of the Galois group
\mathrm{Gal}(\bar{k}/k)
on the irreducible components of
E
. We now assume in addition that all the irreducible components of
E
are defined over the base field
k
and isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{k}
.
16
16

16

Over a field of characteristic zero, this amounts to restricting to ”split” Du Val singularities
A_{n}^{-}
,
D_{n}^{-}
,
E_{6}^{-}
,
E_{7}
and
E_{8}
, see
[
64
]
.

For such singularities, the closed pair
(X,E)
satisfies the assumptions in
5.4.1
,
and the punctured tubular neighborhood
\operatorname{TN}^{\times}_{S}(X_{0},x)
of
x
in
X_{0}
is a natural invariant of the
Nisnevich germ of
x
in
X_{0}
which, by
4.1.8
,
can be computed as the punctured tubular neighborhood
\operatorname{TN}^{\times}_{S}(X,E)
.
Applying
5.4.2
, we obtain the following

Proposition 5.4.3
.

With the assumption above,
the punctured tubular neighborhood
\operatorname{TN}^{\times}_{k}(X_{0},x)

is isomorphic to

\mathbf{1}_{k}\oplus\mathrm{hofib}(\mu(\Gamma))\oplus\mathbf{1}_{k}(2)[3]

Here
\mu(\Gamma)
is the square matrix with entries in
\GW(k)
obtained from the integer valued intersection matrix
(E_{i},E_{j})_{i,j}
associated to the Dynkin diagram
\Gamma=A_{n},D_{n},E_{6},E_{7},E_{8}
by replacing each diagonal entry
-2
by
-h
.

The above proposition implies that the
stable motivic link

\operatorname{TN}^{\times}(\Gamma):=\operatorname{TN}^{\times}_{k}(X_{0},x)
of the Du Val singularity
germ
(X,x_{0})
depends only on the Dynkin diagram
\Gamma
.
We summarize these links in

.
